@@543504 <h> Connect with Us <h> Citizen scientists help reforest kelp on ocean floor <p> Michael Mehta , professor of geography and environmental studies at Thompson Rivers University and coordinator of the Help the Kelp project , drops a carboy of spore-filled kelp strips into Gabriola Island 's Clark Bay . Volunteer Paul O'Sullivan assists . The two hope the project will replenish kelp in the area . <p> Michele Fire-River Heart worked quickly in the chilly morning air , peeling strips of spore-filled kelp off paper backing and punching them into plastic carboys . The ocean at her back was smooth . Too smooth . There was a time , decades ago , when the bay would have been dense with the bobbing heads of bull kelp . Today , only a few lone stems weave up from the rocky ocean bottom . <p> " Kelp is the forest of the ocean .... and it 's disappearing , " said Heart , from Clark Bay on Gabriola Island . " People that have lived here for 20 years said kelp was so thick you could n't row a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ patches . I want to help change that . " <p> Heart is part of a team of ' Help the Kelp ' citizen scientists staging a coastal intervention to fight the disappearance of underwater forests . <p> Floating bull kelp canopies are considered critical ocean habitat and a major engine for near-shore productivity , providing a food source for urchins and crabs and shelter for juvenile fish . They absorb carbon dioxide and are believed to slow down shore erosion by softening the power of waves . Ecologists call kelp the unsung hero of the underwater food web and say its loss is a growing concern . Some question whether the decline of species like salmon is because of the waning underwater habitat . <p> Washington State 's Department of Natural Resources has been tracking the health of kelp since 1989 and said it appears there are canopy losses in inland waters , near the eastern Juan de Fuca Strait and Puget Sound . Historical records show there used to be large floating beds -- the majority of which no longer exist . <p> Helen Berry , marine ecologist in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said it 's important experts on both sides of the border investigate the causes of disappearing underwater forests and what can be done to turn the tide . <p> The challenge for restoration groups is the algae is not yet fully understood and there are still questions around its natural life cycle and factors affecting development . There are also data gaps on the B.C. coast , because the provincial government does n't consistently track or manage bull kelp habitat . It says environmental monitoring on issues like birds , kelp and amphibians is best left to citizen groups . <p> " We have seen lots of cuts in recent years in terms of environmental monitoring and basic science that allows us to address questions we need answers to , " said Colin Bates , professor of life sciences at Quest University . <p> And finding solutions to kelp bed losses is important , he added . <p> " It 's like saying , what will happen if we cut down all the trees ? If we lose bull kelp habitat you lose the value and structural complexity of that area @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from coastlines is a mystery , according to marine ecologists . <p> There is speculation the bulbous algae has been affected by shoreline development , nutrient pollution , climate change and increased vessel traffic . The food web has also changed in places that have been over-fished or seen declined numbers of predators , leaving kelp grazer populations of urchins and crabs unchecked . <p> Some experts , like B.C. Parks aquatic ecologist Doug Biffard , believe kelp beds go through fluxes , making it difficult to tell if declines are because of human activity or natural cycles . <p> Whatever the reason , advocates are now questioning how they can restore the canopies . So far , experiments have shown only limited success . <p> " It 's not like restoring a salmon stream where you go to the library and borrow 15 books that explain the ecology , how habitat functions and what changes you can do ... 50 years of work went into figuring that out , " said Brian Allen , an ecologist with the Washington-based Puget Sound Restoration Fund . " <p> A cross-border team effort is taking place to find solutions to the disappearing kelp forests . <p> No one has found the magic bullet yet , but with each group working on a different piece of the puzzle , Allen believes it 's only a matter of time . <p> The Puget Sound Restoration Fund , for example , has taken a step back from replanting work to investigate the life cycle of bull kelp . While ecologists understand how kelp functions in a lab , they do n't know the timing and progression of the algae in the wild , Allen said . <p> The Washington Department of Natural Resources believes it might also have something to contribute with the results of long-term monitoring . It has tracked bull and giant kelp along the Juan de Fuca Strait and outer coastline for more than two decades and has found a " significant increase " in kelp closest to the open ocean . There also seems to be a link in weather . In 1997 during one of the region 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its lowest kelp yields compared to a La Nina year in 2000 when kelp seemed to flourish . The work is helping scientists understand the overall trend in kelp and what leads to the potential success in some areas and decline in others , Berry said . <p> Help the Kelp volunteers are working to create their own stamp on reforestation efforts . They are documenting efforts to create an affordable , community-based solution to raising kelp that other groups can carry out . <p> It launched its third test project last week to start filling in gaps in the kelp ecosystem around Gabriola Island . A Google Earth mapping project earlier this year showed volunteers where kelp was most sparse . <p> Paul O'Sullivan , a volunteer , called the pilot project an exciting initiative . It is being done for pure reasons and " we are playing a small part in what may be in the future ... a very , very important effort , " he said . <p> On Monday , volunteers replanted the second wave of kelp in Clark Bay . Weighted by a cement block on one end and a buoy on the other , the sori-filled carboys are anticipated to release tiny spores onto the ocean floor . There they will reproduce and begin to grow , creating a new forest of kelp . <p> Dr. Michael Mehta , professor of geography and environmental studies at Thompson Rivers University and coordinator of Help the Kelp , said the group is hopeful this process will work because it seems to mimic the natural life cycle . <p> " What we are doing is kind of like a hair transplant .... spores are moved around from dense areas to less dense areas , " he said . <p> The group will know next spring whether its idea has taken root . @@543589 <h> IPCC 2013 : Carbon dioxide a key question for major climate report <p> Scientists are more confident than ever that pumping carbon dioxide into the air by burning fossil fuels is warming the planet . By how much is something governments and scientists meeting in Stockholm will try to pin down with as much precision as possible Friday . <p> By:Karl RitterThe Associated Press , Published on Thu Sep 26 2013 <p> STOCKHOLM -- Scientists are more confident than ever that pumping carbon dioxide into the air by burning fossil fuels is warming the planet . The question is , by how much ? <p> It 's something that officials and scientists meeting in Stockholm will try to pin down as precisely possible Friday in a seminal report on global warming . <p> Future global warming levels depend on two major factors . One is how much more carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) and other greenhouse gases are pumped into the air and how quickly . <p> The other is the rate at which those gases cause warming , sort of like a revving car engine . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ scientists are trying to figure out how much warming would happen with different levels of carbon pollution . The higher the climate sensitivity or rate , the higher the warming per ton of greenhouse gas emitted . <p> The values adopted by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change are important because they could affect how hard governments try to rein in CO2 emissions -- which are still going up largely due to the rapid expansion of China and other emerging economies . <p> A lower value may reduce the world 's sense of urgency in making a costly energy transformation from oil , coal and gas to renewable sources like solar or wind power -- or in halting the destruction of the Earth 's forests , which capture CO2 . <p> " It 's a key part of the climate problem , " said Chris Field , a Carnegie Institution scientist who is a leader in the IPCC but was n't involved in the report due Friday . <p> The greenhouse effect , which explains how CO2 and other greenhouse gases trap heat in the atmosphere , was discovered in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The IPCC is expected to say Friday that it 's 95 per cent certain that more than half the surface warming of the Earth that has been observed since 1951 is due to the CO2 emissions resulting from human activities . <p> Climate sensitivity is a measure of how much temperatures would go up if the concentration of CO2 doubled in the atmosphere . <p> In its third assessment , the IPCC estimated this as a likely range of 1.5-4.5 degrees Celsius ( 2.7-8.1 Fahrenheit ) . The fourth report , in 2007 , raised the lower end of that range to 2 C ( 3.6 F ) , and gave a best estimate of 3 C ( 5.4 F ) . <p> Now , new research has shown that the upward move may have been too hasty , and there 's a discussion in Stockholm over whether to bring the lower end back down to 1.5 C ( 2.7 F ) . <p> It may seem like a minor detail , but it makes a difference to governments , which want to know how much CO2 emissions need @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2 C ( 3.6 F ) compared to before humans started burning fossil fuels . That 's the limit they have agreed to in UN climate talks . Temperatures have already gone up about 0.8 C ( 1.4 F ) . <p> Reducing the lower range of climate sensitivity " would mean that we have a better chance of staying below 2 degrees than we thought before , " said Kaisa Kosonen , a Greenpeace climate activist . " But I would n't bet on it because they are not lowering the higher end of the range . " <p> In leaked comments on a June draft of the IPCC report , the British government called climate sensitivity " a key issue of concern " that helps give policy-makers a sense of how big a threat climate change is . <p> The United States , Australia and Norway have called for the authors to provide a single value as their best estimate in addition to a range , to give policy-makers better guidance . <p> " You do n't need to have the last decimal to see that the overall number @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ comment emailed by her spokesman . <p> Some scientists resist giving a single value because it could give the false impression that there 's more certainty than there really is about how sensitive the climate is to CO2 . That does n't mean they doubt that CO2 serves as an engine of warming -- the question is whether it 's a four-cylinder or a V8 . <p> " We know a great deal of the mechanism by which CO2 causes warming , " said Field , the Carnegie scientist . " There is still uncertainty about how much a range of feedbacks either amplify or suppress that warming . " 
@@545956 <p> A new landmark report says it is more certain than ever that pollution from burning fossil fuels is changing earth 's climate and contributing to rising seas , stronger storms , hotter days and severe droughts . <p> The report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change , a United Nations-sponsored panel , also says that global warming is " unequivocal " and warns that limiting climate change will require " substantial and sustained " reductions of greenhouse gas emissions . <p> The 36-page assessment was presented by panel chair Rajendra K. Pachauri and World Metrological Office 's secretary general Michel Jarraud in Stockholm on Friday . <p> " Many of the extremes of the last decade were unprecedented , " said Jarraud at a press conference packed with more than 200 journalists from all over the world . <p> Each of the last three decades have been successively warmer at the earth 's surface than any preceding decade since 1850 , he pointed out . <p> New , improved climate models suggest that temperatures are likely to rise by two degrees Celsius , and sea levels @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of this century , says the report . <p> Among other things , the report says heatwaves are likely to occur more frequently and will last longer . Wet regions will receive more rainfall , dry regions will get less rainfall . <p> Homo sapiens , more than anything else , are to blame for all that . <p> " Human influence has been detected in warming of the atmosphere and the ocean , in changes in the global water cycle , in reductions in snow and ice , in global mean sea level rise , and in changes in some climate extremes , " the report said . <p> It says its scientists are 95 per cent certain that humans are " dominant cause " of global warming since the 1950s . <p> In its previous assessment in 2007 , the panel said it was " very likely " that global warming was man-made . <p> In Toronto , P.J. Partington , Pembina Institute 's climate change policy analyst , said the report has laid out a stark choice : " If we continue on the current trajectory , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> There is another scenario , " one where we can reduce pollution . . . but Ottawa is doing totally the opposite , " said Partington . " We are completely out of step with the world on climate change . If we want to avoid risks , we will need government action . " <p> He said he did not expect Ottawa to change its stance on the Keystone XL pipeline even though the IPCC report primarily blames fossil fuels for pollution and hence , change in climate . <p> " Ottawa is very invested in it . " <p> ( On Thursday , Prime Minister Stephen Harper was in New York City where he said in an interview that Canada will " not take no for an answer " on Keystone even as the U.S. administration continues to weigh whether to approve the project . ) <p> Friday 's IPCC report also said that climate change will also hit the poorest countries the most . <p> The world 's top scientists have been in Stockholm since Monday , thrashing out the final details of this summary report @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Stockholm indicate that government officials haggled with the scientists long into Thursday night over the exact wording of the new report . <p> The summary was released on Friday ; a larger report will be released on Monday and will include comprehensive details and implications of climate change for countries . <p> More than 800 scientists , 9,200 papers and 54,000 comments from 1,000 experts from 55 countries were involved in this report . <p> The other two parts of this assessment report -- likely to run into thousands of pages -- will come next March and April , using a similar process from a four-day meeting of scientists leading to a summary document and then a full report published later . Those parts will examine the probable effects of climate change , and the ways in which the world can tackle the problem . <p> The final report encompassing all three parts will be ready by October 2014 ; it will then be presented at a conference in Copenhagen . <p> Reports by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change , or the IPCC , which came into being in 1988 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ considered the authoritative assessment of global climate risks . <p> An assessment has come out every six or seven years . <p> This report is the first since 2007 and , like the first four , will be used as the ultimate authority on climate science , which will in turn guide critical worldwide negotiations on emissions policy . <p> The panel has helped governments understand the scope of global warming and prepare for rising seas , droughts , hurricanes , flooding and other such extreme weather events . It even shared the Nobel Peace Prize with Al Gore in 2007 . <p> The last report came out in 2007 and was considered depressing . <p> It said emissions of greenhouse gases will further change climate over the next century . It will lead to sea levels rising over the next 100 years by about half a metre , snow will disappear from all but the highest mountains , deserts will spread , oceans will become acidic , leading to the destruction of coral reefs , and deadly heatwaves will become more prevalent . <p> This latest assessment may be IPCC @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Star reported on Thursday that governments are quietly reviewing the working of the panel . <p> Governments are likely looking at its efficacy , its mandate and whether it needs to be changed , John M.R. Stone , an adjunct professor at Carleton University , said in an interview . <p> " It 's a full-scale review . . . as I understand , the governments are taking a close look at how the IPCC works , " said Stone . <p> " It 's appropriate . The IPCC is 25 years old and the landscape has changed considerably since . " <p> But the panel has n't been without controversies . <p> In 2010 , the panel admitted an error in its report on the rate of retreat of Himalayan glaciers . It gave climate skeptics ammunition and they pounced on it and questioned the integrity of the IPCC . 
@@546098 <h> The tar sands do n't have to pollute the water . So why do they ? Add to ... <p> In Canada , water is a key aspect of our collective identity . Our geography and history are shaped by water -- the water that flows in winding rivers , over spectacular waterfalls , crashes onto shorelines , and rests in lakes that defy logic in their size , clarity , and turquoise depths . No matter where you live , chances are that you have powerful memories attached to at least one of Canada 's unique waterscapes . <h> Video <h> Video <p> Water is also a shared resource in Canada . Something we all enjoy without significant cost and with confidence that it is clean and safe ( with some notable exceptions , particularly in First Nations communities ) . We use it on a daily basis to cook with , swim in , grow our food , and drink . While many of us are doing our part to keep water safe and clean , it 's a different story when it comes to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ spends tens of millions of dollars bombarding Canadians with ads on TV , online , and in print , trying to convince us that the tar sands are not harming our water . Unfortunately , nothing could be further from the truth and Canadians deserve a reality check when it comes to the impact of tar sands extraction on our water . <p> The process of extracting , processing and transporting bitumen requires enormous quantities of water . In 2011 alone the tar sands used 170 million cubic metres of water , equivalent to the residential use of 1.7 million Canadians . Most of this water is being taken from the Athabasca River , a massive tributary of the Mackenzie , one of the largest and most ecologically significant rivers in the world . The lack of ecologically based regulations regarding extraction rates means tar sands developers could continue to withdraw water from the Athabasca until the river can no longer support the ecosystems and life that depend on it . <p> Historically , the development of the tar sands industry has been made possible and profitable through the development of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not kept pace with the increases in extraction rates . Technology exists , for example , to generate far less liquid tailings waste , yet companies continue to generate over 200 million litres of this toxic liquid each day . These tailings leak about 11 million litres of toxic waste into the river and surrounding land each and every day -- over the course of a year that is enough toxic waste to fill Toronto 's Rogers Centre two and a half times . <p> Often the public is told about how new rules and standards will better protect the health of local ecosystems and people , but not if they are being largely ignored . For example , between 2011 and 2012 , not a single company was in compliance with Alberta 's Directive 074 , which requires a modest progress in accelerating the cleanup of tailings waste . The Provincial government has done nothing to punish the industry for breaking the rules -- therefore rendering them toothless . <p> Findings from research and expert review panels have also heavily criticized the monitoring of tar sands impacted regions , finding @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and industry can not accurately measure and monitor the impact of tar sands extraction on water resources . Research done by Erin Kelly and David Schindler of the University of Alberta , for example , concluded that the current monitoring system has " serious defects . " In total , the absence of effective enforcement and monitoring rewards lack of industry investment in environmental management . It allows industry to avoid paying the real cost of using and polluting water and it encourages tar sands companies to treat Canada 's shared water as their personal resource and a toxic dumping ground . <p> Fortunately , the tar sands industry and its regulators are increasingly under a microscope as they face growing questions from scientists and concerned citizens . There is also growing scrutiny from major trading partners like the United States who are rightfully concerned that the Canadian tar sands are polluting water and air at the same time that industry wants to expand them and sell to new markets . <p> As a result it is becoming harder to deny the devastating impacts of rapid tar sands growth on nearby @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in climate change-causing pollution . All these impacts are being driven by the burning of fossil fuels and it has never been clearer that we need a plan to phase them out and develop safe , clean and renewable energy . While we work towards this brighter energy future , we also need to ensure that current tar sands operations are doing as little damage to our shared environment as possible . <p> Improvements in dealing with tailings and reducing water use and pollution are obvious places to make meaningful change . The technology is available and could be implemented much more ambitiously . Instead of bombarding Canadians with a bonanza of pro-tar sands ads that desperately try to paint the tar sands green , the industry should roll out plans that actually make a difference .

@@546158 <h> Jacques Monod : a scientist whose revolution is still unfolding Add to ... <p> As a scientist , Sean B. Carroll works at the frontiers of " evo-devo , " the field that seeks to trace the evolutionary history of life by looking at how organisms develop . As an author , he has chronicled the discoveries and epic voyages of Charles Darwin , Alfred Russel Wallace and other titans of his field . <p> In this image made available by the Oregon Health &amp; Science University , a faint white blotch in the tube at right is DNA that has been removed from a human egg , center . The red dot is from a laser used in the procedure Oregon Health &amp; Science University <h> video <h> video <p> In Brave Genius , his latest book , Dr. Carroll introduces readers to French scientist Jacques Monod , a seminal figure in molecular biology . The book uncovers new details about Mr. Monod 's life , including harrowing times in the French Resistance and his close friendship with writer Albert Camus . It portrays a researcher @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while engaging with the political tumult of his era . <p> The Globe and Mail caught up with Dr. Carroll ahead of his keynote address at this weekend 's Toronto Science Festival . <p> How do you decide what to write about ? <p> My whole approach is storytelling . I really believe in storytelling as a way of communicating science and showing that science is done by real people who experience real life like the rest of us -- in the case of Jacques Monod , sometimes more real than most of us experience . <p> What was Dr. Monod 's contribution to science ? <p> Dr. Monod and his close collaborator Fran ? ois Jacob were really the French equivalent of Watson and Crick . General audiences know about Watson and Crick because they discovered the structure of DNA . But it was Monod and Jacob who provided the first really lucid glimpse of how genes work . <p> How did they do it ? <p> They found the first genetic switch , and called it that . Whether or not certain genes are switched on is the way @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ right enzyme at the right time , or the way a cell " knows " what kind of cell to become , or the way an embryo knows how to develop . As early as 1947 , Dr. Monod understood that he was seeing the simplest version of the complex and extremely mysterious way that life works . And while he and Dr. Jacob sorted that out , all the tools and tricks they developed laid the foundations for biotechnology . <p> How did his time in the French Resistance affect his life as a scientist ? <p> Dr. Monod 's experience with the Nazi occupation made him desperate to return to science when the war was over . But he also felt that scientists had a responsibility to speak up and work against dangerous ideology . He did so during the resistance and then later as a public opponent of Soviet totalitarianism . I was astounded to learn , for example , that at the most intense phase of his scientific life , when he and Dr. Jacob were doing the work that was going to win them the Nobel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trying to get two scientists out of Hungary in a cloak-and-dagger operation . <p> Do you think scientists today should be so outspoken ? <p> Absolutely . I do n't think as a scientist you 're obliged to , but because scientists have a knowledge base and a system of testing what 's true , they 're valued . That does n't mean scientists should have an exclusive voice , because controversial issues are often driven by other human concerns and scientists can also have conflicts of interest . But not to have scientists at the table when we 're talking about things like climate change , vaccines or GMOs , I think that 's flying blind . <p> What part did Albert Camus play in shaping Dr. Monod 's ideas ? <p> I think there 's evidence that Camus had a big influence on Dr. Monod , who embraced his view of the world and added a scientific dimension to it . Mr. Camus 's philosophy is explicitly about relying on reason and the idea that our lives are what we make of them . Dr. Monod thought the new @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ omnipotent role that chance has played in the history of life on Earth . As he put it , all innovation in the biosphere was dependent on this chance process of changing DNA . <p> Today , the medical breakthroughs that flow from genetics are widely valued . Yet many people still have trouble with evolution , which is all about the action of genes over time . Why the disconnect ? <p> Dr. Monod said that society had accepted science 's treasures but not its more profound message . He was worried about that and he thought it meant trouble ahead . It 's paradoxical that science has blazed forward and produced so much usable knowledge , but some of it makes people uncomfortable and so they ignore it -- but that does n't make it less true . <p> In addition to your own research , you lead a science education effort through the Howard Hughes Medical Institute . What are you trying to achieve there ? <p> I 've been interested in doing something about the landscape of science education in the United States . In my own @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ know and how we know it into the classroom -- especially for students 12 to 22 . I 'm not sure there 's a lot of changing of minds that goes on later in life . But exposing kids to the process of science , to the discoveries of science , to the lives of scientists -- I think that has some potential . <p> Ivan Semeniuk is The Globe and Mail 's science reporter . This interview has been edited and condensed . <h> Restrictions <p> All rights reserved . @@550980 <h> Canada to feel impact of rising sea levels : U.N report <p> As temperatures and sea levels rise around the world , Canada will feel the impact , says a full report by a group of the world 's top climate scientists . <p> Monsoon patterns will change globally , triggering intense tropical cyclones along the western coast of the U.S. and Mexico , and along the eastern coast of U.S. and Canada . <p> The report also says that warming near the Great Lakes , which have lost more than 70 per cent of ice cover between 1973 and 2010 , could be 50 per cent more than what is predicted globally . <p> The 2,216-page report was released on Monday by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change , a United Nations-sponsored panel that looked at previous trends and makes predictions based on climate models . <p> Among other things , it says that melting glaciers , especially in Alaska and the Arctic , have contributed to rising sea levels . It also noted that snowfall in Canada has increased in the north but says there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ part of the country between 1950 and 2009 . <p> On Friday , the summary of the report was released . <p> It reiterated that pollution from burning fossil fuels is changing the earth 's climate and contributing to rising seas , stronger storms , hotter days and severe droughts . <p> The assessment also said that global warming is " unequivocal " and warns that limiting climate change will require " substantial and sustained " reductions of greenhouse gas emissions , which are reaching a tipping point . <p> " Many of the extremes of the last decade were unprecedented , " said Michel Jarraud , Michel Jarraud , secretary general of the World Meteorological Oragnization , adding that each of the last three decades has been successively warmer at the earth 's surface than any preceding decade since 1850 . <p> In the northern hemisphere , the period between 1983 and 2012 was likely the warmest 30-year period of the last 1,400 years . <p> It was n't starkly different from the previous assessment , published in 2007 ; there was just more evidence , both of climate change @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are 95 per cent certain that humans are the " dominant cause " of global warming since the early 1950s . <p> In its previous assessment in 2007 , the panel said it was " very likely " that global warming was manmade . <p> The report is the most thorough assessment of the issue yet and it says that new , improved climate models and better understanding of climate system response have helped it come to the conclusions that temperatures are likely to increase by two degrees Celsius -- and up to 4.8 degrees if emissions remain high -- and sea levels will possibly rise by almost one metre by 2100 . <p> The report also says heat waves are likely to occur more frequently and will last longer . Wet regions will receive more rainfall , dry regions will get less . Greenland and Antarctic are losing mass ; glaciers are shrinking almost worldwide . <p> Reports by the IPCC , which came into being in 1988 , are considered the authoritative assessment of global climate risks . An assessment has come out every six or seven years . 
@@551051 <h> Europe 's green movement fading after a string of election setbacks <p> The anti-nuclear environmentalist movement that burst into politics in the 1980s as a youthful third force has suffered a string of setbacks in Germany and France , raising questions over its future . <p> Germany 's Greens , trailblazers of political ecology , lost ground in a Sept. 22 general election , finishing fourth on 8.4 percent behind the radical Left party , and failing to secure the coalition they wanted with the centre-left Social Democrats . The party 's senior leaders resigned . <p> The Greens are now agonising over whether to enter a coalition with Chancellor Angela Merkel 's conservatives , while their French cousins are feuding over whether to stay in government with President Francois Hollande 's Socialists . <p> One of the French party 's former presidential candidates , Noel Mamere , walked out in disgust last week , saying the Greens had sold their souls for power , stopped producing innovative ideas and become a " trade union of elected officials " . <p> To a degree , the Greens @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all green now <p> " The ecologists spend their time accepting things that do n't correspond to the platform they are supposed to stand for , " Mamere said in an interview with Le Monde . <p> Another icon of the movement , Daniel Cohn-Bendit , distanced himself from the party and wo n't run again for the European Parliament next year , while the current party leader , Pascal Durand , said he would step down in 2014 . <p> The French Greens ' candidate for president , Eva Joly , polled a humiliating 2.3 percent last year , a far cry from the party 's record 16.7 percent score in the 2009 European elections . <p> ALL GREEN NOW <p> To a degree , the Greens have become victims of their own success . We are all green now . <p> Many of the issues they forced onto Europe 's policy agenda -- from renewable energy to sustainable development or gay rights -- have been embraced by the mainstream parties , at least in part . <p> Fighting climate change , reducing carbon emissions and recycling waste are standard @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ zeal than the Greens demand . <p> U.S. President Barack Obama and the European Commission both espouse " green growth " as the way to revive industrialised Western economies . <h> Related <p> Merkel 's centre-right government robbed the ecologists of probably their most potent issue when she decided to phase out nuclear power plants in Germany after the Fukushima reactor disaster in Japan in 2011 . <p> In France , the Greens lost credibility with many supporters because they failed to make Hollande significantly reduce the country 's dependency on atomic energy . The government plans to shut down just one of 58 reactors in its 5-year term , and even that will take years to dismantle . <p> Likewise , Finland 's Greens have been damaged by staying in a coalition that decided to build new nuclear power stations . <p> Battles against new airports or rail hubs or against the use of hydraulic fracking to search for shale gas have mobilised local support for Green causes , often in alliance with middle-class " not in my back yard " campaigners . <p> But barring another Fukushima nearer to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it is hard to see what would prompt a major revival of the Green movement . <p> In the few places like Austria where Greens are still on the rise , it is because they have stayed in opposition to left-right " grand coalitions " and look clean amid others ' sleaze scandals . <p> " FAIR WEATHER " MOVEMENT ? <p> Generation change has also caught up with the Greens . <p> The activists in jeans and T-shirts whose playful insolence blew a gust of fresh air into parliaments and local assemblies across Europe in the 80s and 90s have become pillars of the establishment in many countries . <p> Green ministers have held cabinet seats in Paris , Berlin and half a dozen other EU capitals on-and-off since the late 1990s -- and not just for the environment . <p> Joschka Fischer , once an anarchist firebrand , was a widely admired German foreign minister in 1998-2005 , who justified his country 's first participation in military action since World War Two in NATO 's campaign in Kosovo in 1999 . <p> Ecologists sit on regional executives and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ prosperous German state , Baden-Wuerttemberg , even has a Green premier . <p> But there is a flipside to such success . Green members and voters have aged with their leaders and become more bourgeois and reconciled with the market economy . Surveys show they have a higher than average education level and income . <p> The German Greens alienated some of that electorate by campaigning for higher taxes , prompting Fischer to observe that the party leadership had " gotten older but not yet grown up " . <p> Drawing lessons from defeat , new parliamentary floor leader Katrin Goering-Eckardt said : " Our task is to make ourselves more able to connect with the middle ground in society . " <p> That , however , makes the party less attractive to young people seeking a radical alternative in a period of economic crisis and mass youth unemployment . <p> From Greece to Italy , and Ireland to Spain , disaffected youngsters have either turned apolitical or preferred radical anti-austerity parties of the far left or hard right . In Italy , the anti-establishment 5-Star Movement that surged to prominence @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Single-issue groups such as the Pirates , who campaign for Internet freedom , and eurosceptical parties have drawn some first-time voters away from the Greens , who are strongly pro-European at a time when EU institutions have been tarnished . <p> The Greens ' failure to capitalise on the global financial crisis or the euro zone 's debt woes suggests they are seen as a " fair weather " party , advocating policies that seem a luxury in tough economic times . <p> Despite scientific reports on the devastating march of man-made global warming , the public appetite for carbon taxes , wind turbines and recycling biomass has diminished . <p> In the see-saw between industrial efficiency and environmental sustainability , the balance has swung for now towards short-term economic interests .

@@551426 <h> CTV Investigates : Are You Covered ? <p> With massive floods in Calgary and Toronto this year leading the way , Canadians have claimed more than $5 billion in flood-related damage . <p> But if not for senior levels of government stepping in to foot the bill , many of those claims would have gone denied -- as Canada stands along among G8 countries as the only nation not to offer insurance in case of overland flooding . <p> Most Canadian home insurance policies cover flooding only in cases of sewage system backups . <p> The Insurance Bureau of Canada sees no reason to advocate for overland flood insurance , telling CTV News in a statement that it would disproportionately hit Canadians who would never need it . <p> " Overland flooding is a risk for only the small percentage of the population who live in floodplains or flood-prone areas , " the organization says . <p> " Since most homeowners are n't exposed to this risk , they should n't be expected to share in the cost . " <p> The bureau also notes that in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in overland flooding insurance programs . <p> Along the Nith River in New Hamburg is one area where homeowners are regularly exposed to risks of overland flooding . <p> Although there is little flood damage most years , that was n't the case in 2008 . <p> That year , water levels in the basement of Phylis Sisco 's home on Grace Street peaked at about one foot -- and remained there for the better part of a week . <p> After that , Sisco and her husband installed three sump pumps to ensure they would n't get caught by overland flooding again . <p> Even with the sump pumps , the Siscos -- who have lived on Grace Street for 44 years -- have learned not to take any chances when flood warnings are issued . <p> " We get the covers all off and we get prepared . We pick up things in our basement , " she says . <p> Despite not wanting to adopt overland flooding insurance , the insurance industry is n't completely idle on the matter of floods . <p> University
